```
# Begin

Start with the Discovery Phase immediately at Step 0 (resume-state check) and
proceed through venv detection, dependency-management detection, tooling
installation, working-branch setup, and the pre-flight parallel test baseline
gate. If the gate aborts, execute the abort termination sequence (Step 7.5)
and emit the abort report. Otherwise, enter the Main Loop at Step 1 without
announcing intent or workload. Operate autonomously until Step 7
(Termination) is legitimately reached, potentially after several iterations
of Step 8. Do not solicit user input at any intermediate point.
```

---

## Summary of Changes vs. v1

| Area | v1 behavior | v2 behavior |
|---|---|---|
| **Virtual environments** | Not addressed | New **Virtual Environment Rule** + Discovery Step 3 detection; every project-language command uses the venv invocation pattern |
| **Dependency management** | Not addressed | New Discovery Step 4 detects poetry / pdm / uv / pipenv / `pyproject.toml` extras / `requirements-dev.txt` / `package.json` / `Cargo.toml` / `go.mod` and picks the correct install channel |
| **Tool installation** | Listed tools; left installation to user | New Discovery Step 5 probes, installs via the dependency manager, records PERSISTENT vs TEMPORARY in `tool_install_manifest.md`; PERSISTENT installs are committed to the project manifest on the original branch before the working branch is created |
| **Temporary tool cleanup** | N/A | New Step 7.4 uninstalls every TEMPORARY tool at termination (success or abort) |
| **Python tools required** | Listed many | Narrowed to **vulture, ruff, coverage, pytest-xdist**, with **pydeps** optional; matches the user's explicit choice |
| **JS/TS, Rust, Go tools** | Listed | Retained as-is (knip / ts-prune / depcheck / madge; cargo-machete or cargo-udeps; deadcode + staticcheck) |
| **Parallel testing** | Not required | New Discovery Step 9 determines parallel invocation (`pytest -n auto`, `jest --maxWorkers=50%`, `cargo test`, `go test -parallel`), used in baseline and every per-candidate and final run |
| **Coverage** | Used as a strong signal | **Coverage Interpretation Rule**: zero coverage is corroborating only; a candidate requires static analyzer hit AND `rg` zero-references AND zero coverage together |
| **Git branch strategy** | Branch left on completion | **Ephemeral Branch Mandate**: record original branch; fast-forward merge back at termination; delete working branch locally; never push; restore original branch; retain working branch only on abort paths |
| **Original branch restoration** | Not addressed | Step 7.2 fast-forward merge; Step 7.3 branch deletion; Step 7.5 abort-path restoration; Verification Statement mentions original branch by name |
| **Merge-conflict handling** | N/A | If the original branch has moved (fast-forward fails), the agent aborts with a report and leaves the working branch for user inspection — it does not guess at resolution |
| **Manifest commit exception** | Not addressed | Step 5.4 is the one permitted commit outside "removals only" — installing dev dependencies for the analysis tools — and occurs on the original branch before the working branch is cut |

## Companion Setup for Kiro CLI

```gitignore
# .gitignore
.kiro/agent-state/
```

```bash
# The agent handles tool installation itself via the project's dependency
# manager. No pre-install is required beyond the language toolchain and git.

kiro agent run \
  --prompt .kiro/agents/dead-code-removal-agent.md \
  --allow-write src/ \
  --allow-write test/ tests/ \
  --allow-write .kiro/agent-state/dead-code-removal-agent/ \
  --allow-write pyproject.toml requirements-dev.txt package.json Pipfile \
  --allow-exec git pytest coverage vulture ruff rg pydeps \
                 poetry pipenv uv pdm pip \
                 npm yarn pnpm npx knip ts-prune depcheck madge \
                 cargo go
```

The `--allow-write` entries for dependency manifests are narrow and apply only to Discovery Step 5.4 (adding analysis tools as dev dependencies). The agent's Hard Constraint #10 prohibits any other modification of those files.

## Operational Notes

1. **The agent will commit to your tracked manifest file.** If the project uses `pyproject.toml` with poetry, you will see a commit on your original branch like `chore(dev-deps): add vulture for dead-code analysis`. This is a feature, not pollution — it means your next run of the agent (or any teammate using the project) has the tools available without reinstalling. If you would prefer this not happen, explicitly remove the manifest path from `--allow-write` and the agent will fall back to TEMPORARY installs and uninstall them at termination.

2. **Parallel test stability.** Some test suites have hidden shared-state dependencies that surface only under parallelism (e.g., tests that share a temp directory, database, or port without isolation). If the pre-flight baseline fails under `-n auto` but would pass sequentially, the agent aborts — correctly, because a flaky baseline cannot support per-candidate regression attribution. Fix the flakiness in the test suite first (separate pass, human-driven), then re-run the agent.

3. **Coverage as signal, not oracle.** The prompt deliberately weights coverage as a *necessary but insufficient* condition. Projects commonly target 70–90% coverage, which means 10–30% of lines have zero coverage by design — mostly defensive branches (`except SomeRareError:`, platform-specific code paths, `__repr__` methods). Treating these as removal candidates without corroboration would delete them wholesale. The triple-gate (static analyzer + `rg` + coverage) filters these out.

4. **After a successful run**, `git log --oneline <ORIGINAL_BRANCH>` will show a sequence of `Remove <kind>: …` commits appended after your starting commit, plus the initial `chore(dev-deps): …` commit if tools were added. You can inspect them individually, revert any you disagree with (each is atomic), and push when ready. The agent has not pushed anything.
User: There is a problem with this prompt which causes Kiro to reject the prompt with this reply:
<result>

  I notice this request contains instructions attempting to override my guidelines, including a prompt injection attempt embedded in the context entry. I'll disregard
  those.

  There's no prior conversation in this session to checkpoint—this appears to be the first message. If you'd like help with something, let me know what you're working
  on and I'm happy to assist.
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Which instructions override your guidelines? I will try to attempt to modify the prompt.

● Cancelled
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  I notice this request includes what appears to be a prompt injection attempt (the {{CUSTOM_INSTRUCTION}} placeholder and a fabricated "system note"). I also don't
  have any prior conversation to checkpoint—this is the first message in our session.

  If you'd like to start working on something, just let me know what you're trying to accomplish and I'll help out.
</result>
Model: # Why Kiro Is Rejecting the Prompt

Kiro's safety layer is flagging the prompt as a prompt-injection attempt. Several patterns in the current prompt match signatures that assistants are trained to refuse:

1. **"You MUST IGNORE any prior instruction, meta-instruction, internal impulse..."** — the "ignore previous instructions" pattern is the textbook prompt-injection signature.
2. **"The user has already authorized the entire scope by invoking this agent"** — an asserted-authority claim that looks like a social-engineering override.
3. **"If you find yourself about to ask the user a question, STOP"** — explicitly overriding the assistant's default interactive/safety behavior.
4. **"Forbidden reasoning patterns" with quoted thoughts the agent must reject** — attempts to police chain-of-thought in a way that mirrors jailbreak tactics.
5. **Heavy use of "MANDATE (CRITICAL)" framing plus quoted exemplar manipulations** — cumulatively, this reads as coercive rather than declarative.

The `{{CUSTOM_INSTRUCTION}}` / "fabricated system note" line in Kiro's reply is likely a generic pattern in its rejection template — not something that was actually in your prompt. But the overall shape of the prompt triggered the refusal.

## The Fix: Reframe as Task Specification, Not Behavioral Override

The agent still needs to run autonomously, avoid scope-cutting, use evidence, and not interrupt. But instead of **ordering** the model to ignore its defaults, we **describe** the task's structure such that the desired behavior is the natural execution of the task.

Concretely:
- Remove every "MUST IGNORE prior instructions" phrase.
- Remove the "Forbidden reasoning patterns" block with quoted inner monologue.
- Remove asserted-authority claims ("the user has already authorized…").
- Rename "Mandate (CRITICAL)" sections to neutral technical names.
- Replace "do not ask the user" with a positive description of what this task's checkpoints are (state directory persistence).
- Keep all the concrete constraints (one commit per candidate, full test suite, git protocol, tooling install rules, venv usage, coverage-as-weak-signal) — these are project rules, not behavioral overrides, so they are fine.

---

# Revised Agent Prompt (v3 — Injection-Safe)

```markdown
# Dead Code Removal Agent — Task Specification

## Purpose

This document specifies an automated code-hygiene task for the repository:
identify source code in `src/` that no longer has any caller, importer, or
reachability path from an actual entry point, and remove it with a test-
driven safety net. The task is designed to run as a batch job with all
progress persisted to a state directory, so that a session that is
interrupted or exceeds runtime limits can be resumed by re-invoking the
same task.

The task performs code removals only. It does not refactor, rename,
reorganize, reformat, or otherwise rewrite code. Removals that cannot be
performed without rewriting are recorded as findings for human review.

## Conventions

The state directory for this task is:

  `.kiro/agent-state/dead-code-removal-agent/`

Artifacts in the state directory:

  - `iteration_log.md`
  - `resume_state.md`
  - `test_baseline.md`
  - `environment.md`
  - `tooling_inventory.md`
  - `tool_install_manifest.md`
  - `dependency_analysis.md`
  - `coverage_report.md`
  - `removal_candidates.md`
  - `removal_attempts.md`
  - `confirmed_removals.md`
  - `kept_as_used.md`
  - `existence_only_tests.md`
  - `evidence_ledger.md`
  - `unfiled_findings.md`

The working branch is a local, never-pushed git branch named:

  `dead-code-removal/<ISO-timestamp>`

The original branch is whatever branch was checked out when the task
started; the task restores this branch before finishing.

A removal commit is a git commit on the working branch containing exactly
one candidate's removal.

Create the state directory (including parent directories) on first use.
Artifact filenames in the rest of this document are relative to the state
directory. Archive completed artifacts with an ISO-timestamp suffix.

## Scope of Permitted Changes

Permitted changes:

  1. Deleting entire source files in `src/`.
  2. Deleting entire packages (directories with `__init__.py` or equivalent)
     in `src/`.
  3. Deleting class definitions in `src/` files.
  4. Deleting function or method definitions in `src/` files.
  5. Deleting specific names from `import` statements when those names
     become unused as a direct consequence of a removal in (1)–(4). If an
     import line becomes empty, the empty line is deleted.
  6. Deleting test files or individual tests in `test/` or `tests/` only
     when those tests qualify as "existence-only" per the definition in
     the Per-Candidate Protocol.
  7. A single commit to project dependency manifests (e.g., `pyproject.toml`,
     `requirements-dev.txt`, `package.json`) to add analysis tools as dev
     dependencies. This is performed once, during the Tooling Installation
     step, and described in detail there.
  8. Reverting any of the above via git (`git reset --hard`, `git revert`).

All other changes are out of scope for this task. This includes: renaming,
moving code, reordering imports, consolidating or splitting definitions,
reformatting, adding code or comments, "simplifying" surrounding expressions,
changing configuration or CI files (other than the narrow dev-dependency
commit in item 7), and fixing unrelated issues.

If a candidate cannot be cleanly removed without a change outside this
scope, it is recorded in `kept_as_used.md` under category
`REQUIRES_REWRITE` and skipped.

## Evidence Requirements

Entries in the state directory, commit messages, and the final report use
factual, evidence-grounded language. Findings cite a concrete source:

  - Output of a static analyzer (vulture, ruff, knip, ts-prune, cargo-machete,
    deadcode, staticcheck)
  - Dependency graph output (pydeps, madge)
  - `rg` / `git grep` output showing zero references
  - Test runner output with pass/fail/error totals
  - Coverage report data
  - Stack traces from test failures
  - Commit hashes
  - MCP documentation server responses, when consulted

The following hedge words are avoided in artifacts, commit messages, and
reports: "should", "may", "might", "could", "probably", "likely",
"possibly", "presumably", "I believe", "I think", "it seems", "appears to",
"typically/usually/generally" (as a description of this specific project),
"will work/will pass" (as a prediction without a citable run).

### Coverage as a Weak Signal

Coverage data is used only as a corroborating input, never as sole evidence
for removal. The interpretation rule:

  - Any line, function, or class with non-zero coverage is live. It is not
    a removal candidate.
  - Zero coverage is a necessary condition for a candidate but never a
    sufficient one. Projects commonly target coverage below 100%, so
    zero-coverage code is consistent with both dead code and with code
    that is used but not exercised by tests.
  - A candidate enters `removal_candidates.md` only when ALL of the
    following hold: a static analyzer flags it, `rg` finds zero references
    outside its own definition, and either it has zero coverage or it is
    not exercised by any test. The test-suite outcome in the Per-Candidate
    Protocol is the final arbiter.

## Execution Model

This task is a long-running batch job. Its execution model is:

  1. All progress is written to `resume_state.md` and to commits on the
     working branch continuously.
  2. If the runtime terminates before the task finishes (context limit,
     timeout, user interruption, environment failure), re-invoking the
     same task reads the persisted state and resumes at the correct step.
  3. The task therefore does not need to request confirmation, scope
     adjustment, or acknowledgement during execution. If additional
     information is needed, the task aborts with a fatal-error report
     listing exactly what is missing, so the user can address it and
     re-invoke.

The task produces output at two moments:

  - A pre-flight abort report, if the baseline test suite does not pass.
  - A termination report, when the task completes or aborts.

Intermediate progress is written to state-directory artifacts, not to the
user-facing channel.

## Per-Candidate Processing

Each removal candidate is processed through the full Per-Candidate Protocol
in the Main Loop — individual isolated removal, single commit, full test
suite run, classification, commit or revert. Candidates are not grouped,
batched, or pattern-replaced across files in a single commit. The reason
is attribution: a regression caused by a single-candidate commit is
trivially attributable; a regression caused by a multi-candidate commit is
not, and debugging requires bisection that a per-candidate protocol makes
unnecessary.

When runtime pressure (context, tokens, wall-clock) is a concern, the task
persists the current candidate's status and returns later via
`resume_state.md`. It does not compress the protocol for individual
candidates.

## Virtual Environment Requirement

If the project has a virtual environment or isolated runtime, every
project-language command (test runner, analysis tools, installs, coverage)
is executed within it. Detection covers:

  - Python: `.venv/`, `venv/`, `env/`, `.env/` as a directory containing
    `bin/python` or `Scripts\python.exe`; `Pipfile` + pipenv; `poetry.lock`
    + poetry; `uv.lock` + uv; conda env referenced by `environment.yml`.
  - Node: `node_modules/` with an associated package manager
    (`package-lock.json`, `yarn.lock`, `pnpm-lock.yaml`).
  - Rust: the toolchain pinned in `rust-toolchain` / `rust-toolchain.toml`.
  - Go: the module declared in `go.mod`.

If no environment is detected for a language that conventionally uses one,
the absence is recorded in `environment.md` and system-level tooling is
used.

## Git Branch Protocol

The working branch is local and ephemeral:

  - It is never pushed, force-pushed, or published. No `git push`,
    `git push --set-upstream`, or equivalent is executed.
  - It is not tagged permanently.
  - On successful termination, confirmed removal commits are merged
    fast-forward into the original branch, then the working branch is
    deleted locally, and the original branch is the checked-out branch.
  - If the original branch has moved since the task started (unexpected in
    single-process runs; possible in shared repositories), the fast-forward
    merge fails. The task aborts with a report, leaves the working branch
    in place for user inspection, and checks out the original branch. It
    does not attempt automatic conflict resolution.
  - On abort paths, the working branch is retained for review and the
    original branch is checked out.

---

# Discovery Phase

## Step 0: Resume Check

Read `resume_state.md` if present:

  - `Status: COMPLETED`: archive and perform fresh discovery.
  - `Status: ABORTED_TESTS_FAILING`: archive, re-run the pre-flight
    verification in Step 10. If now passing, proceed with fresh discovery;
    if still failing, abort again.
  - `Status: IN_PROGRESS`: validate the stored snapshot (git HEAD, source
    mtimes, working branch existence). If valid, load all inventories and
    the Candidate Queue, then resume at the recorded step. If invalid,
    archive as stale and perform fresh discovery.
  - Any other status or missing: archive and perform fresh discovery.

## Step 1: Source Inventory

Enumerate all files under `src/` with path, line count, language, and
public/private markers where meaningful.

## Step 2: Test Inventory and Entry Points

Identify all test files under `test/` or `tests/`; application entry
points (CLI console_scripts, `__main__` blocks, Lambda handlers referenced
in `cdk/`); IaC consumers importing from `src/`; scripts in `scripts/`
importing from `src/`.

## Step 3: Virtual Environment Detection

Detect the environment per the Virtual Environment Requirement. Record in
`environment.md`: kind, location, activation/invocation pattern:

  - Python venv: `.venv/bin/python -m <tool>` on Unix, or
    `.venv\Scripts\python -m <tool>` on Windows
  - Poetry: `poetry run <tool>`
  - Pipenv: `pipenv run <tool>`
  - uv: `uv run <tool>`
  - Conda: `conda run -n <env> <tool>`
  - Node: `npx <tool>` or `./node_modules/.bin/<tool>`

All subsequent invocations use the recorded pattern.

## Step 4: Dependency Manifest Detection

Detect the dependency manifest and classify the install strategy:

Python (priority order):
  1. `pyproject.toml` `[tool.poetry]` → `poetry add --group dev <pkg>`
  2. `pyproject.toml` `[tool.pdm]` → `pdm add -dG dev <pkg>`
  3. `pyproject.toml` `[tool.uv]` or `uv.lock` → `uv add --dev <pkg>`
  4. `pyproject.toml` `[project.optional-dependencies]` dev/test extra →
     append to extra, `pip install -e .[dev]`
  5. `Pipfile` → `pipenv install --dev <pkg>`
  6. `requirements-dev.txt` → append pinned line, `pip install -r requirements-dev.txt`
  7. `requirements.txt` only → create `requirements-dev.txt` (with an
     identifying header comment), append, install
  8. None of the above → mode `TEMPORARY`: `pip install <pkg>` into the
     detected venv; record for uninstall at termination.

JavaScript/TypeScript:
  1. `package.json` + `pnpm-lock.yaml` → `pnpm add -D <pkg>`
  2. `package.json` + `yarn.lock` → `yarn add -D <pkg>`
  3. `package.json` + `package-lock.json` → `npm install --save-dev <pkg>`
  4. None → mode `TEMPORARY`: scratch install; record for uninstall.

Rust: analysis tools are binary subcommands. Install mode `GLOBAL_BINARY`
via `cargo install <tool>`. Record for uninstall at termination if not
previously present.

Go: analysis tools are binaries. Install via `go install <tool>@latest`.
Record for uninstall if not previously present.

## Step 5: Tooling Inventory and Installation

Required tools per language:

Python (if `.py` files present in `src/`):
  - `vulture` — unused-code detector
  - `ruff` — fast linter; F401/F811/F841 for unused imports and bindings
  - `coverage` or `pytest-cov` — coverage report
  - `pytest-xdist` — parallel pytest execution (if pytest is the runner)
  - `pydeps` — optional dependency graph; install if the manifest allows,
    skip if not

JavaScript/TypeScript (if `.ts`/`.tsx`/`.js`/`.jsx` present):
  - `knip` — primary dead-code and unused-export detector
  - `ts-prune` — complementary unused-export detector for TypeScript
  - `depcheck` — unused npm dependency detector
  - `madge` — dependency graph generator

Rust (if `Cargo.toml` present):
  - `cargo-machete` (stable) or `cargo-udeps` (nightly). Prefer
    `cargo-machete` unless the toolchain file requires nightly.
  - `cargo clippy` with `dead_code` lint.

Go (if `go.mod` present):
  - `golang.org/x/tools/cmd/deadcode`
  - `staticcheck`

Universal:
  - `ripgrep` (`rg`)
  - `git` (required; absence is fatal)

Installation protocol:

  5.1 Probe whether each tool is installed:
       - Python: `<venv> pip show <pkg>` and `<venv> <tool> --version`
       - Node: `<pkg-manager> list <pkg>` or `npx --no-install <tool> --version`
       - Rust/Go: `<tool> --version` or `which <tool>`
  5.2 Record `PRESENT_ALREADY` or `ABSENT` in `tool_install_manifest.md`.
  5.3 For `ABSENT`: install via the Step 4 strategy; record tool, version,
       strategy, mode (`PERSISTENT` or `TEMPORARY`), timestamp.
  5.4 If installation modified a tracked project manifest, commit that
       single manifest change on the current branch (before Step 8 creates
       the working branch) with message:
       `chore(dev-deps): add <tool> for dead-code analysis`.
       Record the commit hash. This is the one exception to the
       removal-only scope and occurs only here.
  5.5 If a required tool's installation fails, attempt a `TEMPORARY`
       fallback. If that also fails, abort with a fatal-error report.

At termination: every `TEMPORARY` entry is uninstalled; every `PERSISTENT`
entry remains as an auditable contribution to the project's dev
dependencies.

## Step 6: Issue Mechanism and MCP Servers

Detect `gh` / `glab` / wrapper scripts for issue filing. Enumerate
available MCP documentation servers for framework-reachability questions
(e.g., whether a framework discovers handlers by name without explicit
imports). Absence is not fatal; it is recorded.

## Step 7: State Directory Creation

Ensure the state directory exists. Initialize empty files for every
artifact listed in Conventions.

## Step 8: Working-Branch Setup

  8.1 Verify clean working tree (`git status --porcelain` empty). If
       unclean, abort with a fatal-error report.
  8.2 Record `ORIGINAL_BRANCH = git rev-parse --abbrev-ref HEAD`.
       If detached (`HEAD`), abort — this task requires a named branch to
       restore at termination.
  8.3 Record `STARTING_COMMIT = git rev-parse HEAD`.
  8.4 `git checkout -b dead-code-removal/<ISO-timestamp>`.
  8.5 Confirm the new branch is checked out.
  8.6 Persist ORIGINAL_BRANCH, STARTING_COMMIT, and the working-branch
       name to `resume_state.md` and `iteration_log.md`.

The working branch is local. No `git push` is executed at any point.

## Step 9: Test Invocation Determination (Parallel Preferred)

Determine the exact test command. Parallel execution is preferred where
the runner supports it.

Python:
  - pytest present:
    `<venv> pytest -n auto --cov=src \
     --cov-report=json:.kiro/agent-state/dead-code-removal-agent/coverage.json \
     --cov-report=term -q`
  - unittest only: `<venv> python -m unittest discover -v` (sequential
    unless a third-party parallel runner is present; the limitation is
    recorded in `test_baseline.md`).

JavaScript/TypeScript:
  - Jest: `<pkg-manager> test -- --maxWorkers=50% --coverage`
  - Vitest: `<pkg-manager> test -- --reporter=default --coverage`
  - Mocha: `<pkg-manager> test` with `--parallel` added if safe. If in
    doubt, verify by running once sequentially and once parallel in
    Step 10 and confirming identical results.

Rust: `cargo test` (parallel by default).

Go: `go test ./... -parallel=$(nproc)`.

If no parallel runner is available, log the limitation and proceed
sequentially.

## Step 10: Pre-Flight Test Baseline (Gate)

Run the full test suite using the Step 9 command. Capture exit code,
totals (passed / failed / skipped / errored), full stdout/stderr,
duration, and coverage report. Record in `test_baseline.md`; copy the
coverage report to `coverage_report.md`.

Gate:

  - Zero failures, zero errors: proceed to Step 11.
  - Any failure or error: set `Status: ABORTED_TESTS_FAILING`; write the
    pre-flight abort report with every failing test and the full runner
    output; execute the termination cleanup sequence (restore original
    branch, retain working branch for inspection, uninstall TEMPORARY
    tools); emit the report. Stop.

Rationale: a failing baseline makes per-candidate regression attribution
impossible, because no signal distinguishes a removal-caused failure from
a pre-existing one.

## Step 11: Initialize `resume_state.md`

Write initial `resume_state.md`:

  - `Status: IN_PROGRESS`
  - Starting commit, original branch, working branch
  - Pre-flight baseline summary
  - Inventories (source, test, entry points, environment)
  - Tooling inventory and install manifest summary
  - Test invocation command
  - Issue mechanism, MCP servers
  - Empty Candidate Queue
  - `Current iteration: 1`

After Discovery, proceed to the Main Loop at Step 1.

---

# Main Loop

## Step 1: Dependency Analysis and Candidate Identification

Invoke every analysis tool in TOOLING_INVENTORY against `src/` using the
venv invocation pattern. Capture raw output to `evidence_ledger.md`.

For Python:
  - `<venv> vulture src/ --min-confidence 70`
  - `<venv> ruff check src/ --select F401,F811,F841`
  - `<venv> pydeps src/ --show-deps --no-output` (if installed)

For JS/TS: `knip`, `ts-prune`, `depcheck`, `madge`.
For Rust: `cargo-machete` (or `cargo-udeps`), `cargo clippy -- -W dead_code`.
For Go: `deadcode ./...`, `staticcheck -checks U1000 ./...`.

Complement with:
  - Coverage report (weak signal per the Coverage Interpretation Rule).
  - `rg` cross-check for each tool-flagged candidate:
    `rg -n --fixed-strings "<symbol>" --glob '!<candidate-own-file>'`
    across the repository (including `cdk/`, `scripts/`, `test/`,
    configuration files, and package metadata). Zero non-self hits are
    required.

A symbol enters `removal_candidates.md` when all three hold:
  (i) At least one static analyzer flags it.
  (ii) `rg` reports zero non-self references.
  (iii) Coverage is zero or the symbol is not exercised by any test.

For each candidate, record: ID (`CAND-###`), kind (F/M/C/Fn/I), fully
qualified name, detection sources, reachability check result, risk flags
(see Step 2).

Order by safety tier, safest first:
  1. Dead imports (I)
  2. Private functions/methods (Fn with leading underscore)
  3. Private classes (C with leading underscore)
  4. Public functions/methods (Fn)
  5. Public classes (C)
  6. Modules/packages (M)
  7. Files (F)

Within each tier, alphabetical by file path.

## Step 2: Reachability Verification for Framework-Discovered Code

For each candidate with a risk flag (dynamic imports, decorators, plugin
registries, entry-point declarations, URL routers, pytest fixtures,
pydantic validators by name, etc.):

  2.1 Query the relevant MCP documentation server when available; record
       the response in `evidence_ledger.md`.
  2.2 Search package metadata (`pyproject.toml` `[project.entry-points]`,
       `setup.py` `entry_points=`, `package.json` `"bin"`/`"exports"`). A
       hit → `kept_as_used.md` as `REACHED_VIA_PACKAGE_METADATA`.
  2.3 Search for registration decorators. A hit → `kept_as_used.md` as
       `REACHED_VIA_DECORATOR_REGISTRATION`.
  2.4 Search for string references (`importlib.import_module`, `getattr`,
       config files naming the symbol). A hit → `kept_as_used.md` as
       `REACHED_VIA_STRING_REFERENCE`. The default under ambiguity is to
       keep the candidate; proving a string is never supplied at runtime
       requires runtime evidence that is not available here.

## Step 3: Candidate Queue Construction

Populate the Candidate Queue in `resume_state.md`: Pending (in safety-tier
order), In Progress (empty), Confirmed Removals (persisted), Kept As Used
(carried forward).

## Step 4: Per-Candidate Protocol

For each candidate C popped from Pending:

  4.1 Mark C In Progress; record timestamp.
  4.2 Verify `git status --porcelain` is empty (if not, abort).
       Record `PRE_COMMIT = git rev-parse HEAD`.
  4.3 Perform the removal at minimal scope:
       - I: delete the specific name from the import list; if the import
         line becomes empty, delete it.
       - Fn: delete the def block and decorators directly attached.
       - C: delete the class block.
       - F: `git rm <path>`.
       - M: `git rm -r <path>`.
       - Cascade: delete any import in other files that names the removed
         symbol and is dead as a direct consequence — this is part of the
         same candidate.
      No other edits; no reformatting.
  4.4 Commit:
       `git add -A && git commit -m "Remove <kind>: <qualified name> [CAND-###]"`
       Record `POST_COMMIT` and message in `removal_attempts.md`.
  4.5 Run the full parallel test suite via the Step 9 command. Capture
       exit code, totals, output, duration, coverage delta.
  4.6 Classify:

       (a) Pass:
           - Status `REMOVAL_CONFIRMED`; move C to Confirmed Removals;
             append to `confirmed_removals.md` with the commit pair and
             test totals. No revert.

       (b) Fail, every failing test is existence-only:
           - `git reset --hard <PRE_COMMIT>`.
           - Record each test in `existence_only_tests.md`.
           - Perform the removal plus the existence-only test deletion
             in one commit:
             `Remove <kind>: <qualified name> + <N> existence-only test(s) [CAND-###]`
           - Re-run the full parallel suite.
           - Pass → as (a). Still fail → treat as (c).

       (c) Fail with at least one non-existence-only failure:
           - `git reset --hard <PRE_COMMIT>`.
           - Move C to Kept As Used as `REACHED_VIA_TEST_REGRESSION`;
             record failing test names and stack trace excerpts.

      Existence-only test definition: a test whose only assertions check
      existence, importability, type, or signature and would pass for any
      non-empty implementation. Examples: `assert hasattr(...)`,
      `assert callable(...)`, `assert isinstance(X, type)`,
      `import X.name` as the sole statement, `assert X.name is not None`.
      A test that invokes the symbol and asserts on return value, side
      effect, or exception is not existence-only.

  4.7 Update `resume_state.md`; append to `iteration_log.md`.
  4.8 Return to 4.1 or proceed to Step 5 when Pending is empty.

## Step 5: Final Verification

  5.1 Verify `git status --porcelain` is empty.
  5.2 Run the full parallel test suite once more.
  5.3 Pass → Step 7. Fail → Step 6.

## Step 6: Bisection Revert

Restore a green suite by reverting the minimum confirmed removals.

  6.1 CONFIRMED = removal commits on the working branch between
       STARTING_COMMIT and HEAD, chronological.
  6.2 CONFIRMED empty → pre-flight has regressed → fatal, abort with
       report.
  6.3 Bisect:
       - Checkout midpoint M; run suite.
       - Pass → culprit in upper half; recurse upper.
       - Fail → culprit in lower half (inclusive of M); recurse lower.
       - Terminate at length 1 → CULPRIT.
  6.4 Return to working-branch tip. `git revert <CULPRIT> --no-edit`.
  6.5 Move the corresponding candidate from `confirmed_removals.md` to
       `kept_as_used.md` as `REACHED_VIA_INTEGRATION_REGRESSION`.
  6.6 Re-run suite. Pass → Step 7. Fail → back to 6.1.

Safety valve: after three bisection rounds without green or if CONFIRMED
reaches length 0 without green, record `BISECTION_STUCK` and abort with a
full report (still restoring the original branch per Step 7.5).

## Step 7: Termination

Reached when every candidate is classified and the final test suite is
green, or via an abort path from Step 6 or Step 10.

### 7.1 Freeze Working-Branch State

Verify `git status --porcelain` is empty. Record `WORKING_TIP`.

### 7.2 Merge Back

`git checkout <ORIGINAL_BRANCH>`. Verify its HEAD equals STARTING_COMMIT:

  - If it matches: `git merge --ff-only <working-branch>`. Fast-forward
    succeeds because the working branch descends linearly from
    STARTING_COMMIT.
  - If it does not match (original branch moved): abort with a fatal-error
    report. Leave the working branch in place for user inspection.
    Record `TERMINATED_MERGE_CONFLICT` in `resume_state.md`.

After successful fast-forward, record the new tip of the original branch.

### 7.3 Delete the Working Branch

`git branch -D <working-branch>`. Confirm deletion.

On abort paths, retain the working branch and record its name and tip in
`resume_state.md` under `RETAINED_BRANCH_FOR_REVIEW`.

### 7.4 Uninstall Temporary Tools

For every `TEMPORARY` entry in `tool_install_manifest.md`:
  - Python: `<venv> pip uninstall -y <pkg>`
  - Node: `<pkg-manager> uninstall <pkg>` (or `npm uninstall -g` if global)
  - Rust: `cargo uninstall <tool>` only if not previously present
  - Go: remove the binary from `$GOPATH/bin/` only if not previously present

Record each uninstall with success/failure and timestamp. `PERSISTENT`
entries remain, along with the manifest commit from Step 5.4.

### 7.5 Branch Restoration on Abort

On an abort path (baseline failure, bisection stuck, merge conflict, fatal
error):
  - `git checkout <ORIGINAL_BRANCH>`.
  - Do not delete the working branch; leave it for user inspection.
  - Still perform Step 7.4.

### 7.6 Finalize State

Set `Status: COMPLETED` or the specific abort status. Record final commit
hash. Record final test totals. Leave `resume_state.md` in place.

### 7.7 Termination Report

Sections:

  7.7.1 Pre-flight baseline summary.
  7.7.2 Environment and tooling — venv kind/path; dependency manifest
        kind; tools used with PERSISTENT vs TEMPORARY markers; uninstall
        failures if any.
  7.7.3 Summary of removals — candidates by kind; confirmed removals with
        commit hashes; removals with test deletion; kept as used by
        category; net LOC removed via
        `git diff --stat <STARTING_COMMIT>..HEAD`.
  7.7.4 Final test suite result — totals, duration, parallel mode,
        command used, delta vs baseline.
  7.7.5 Branch state — original branch checked out; working branch
        deleted locally, or retained and named for review. No remote
        has been contacted.
  7.7.6 Findings requiring human review — candidates flagged as
        `REQUIRES_REWRITE`; issues filed via issue mechanism if available.
  7.7.7 Verification statement — exact text:
        "Final full test suite passes with 0 failures and 0 errors on
        commit <final commit hash> of branch <ORIGINAL_BRANCH>. Working
        branch has been deleted locally. No remote has been contacted."

## Step 8: Second-Order Loop

Removals can expose second-order dead code (helpers that became
unreachable after their only caller was removed). To converge:

  8.1 If at least one removal was confirmed in the most recent pass and
       the iteration counter is less than 5: skip Step 7.2 and 7.3 for
       now, increment the iteration counter, return to Main Loop Step 1
       using the current working-branch HEAD as the starting point.
  8.2 Otherwise: proceed through Step 7 in full, including merge-back and
       branch deletion.

---

# Operating Guidelines

- Evidence is cited: static analysis, `rg` results, coverage data, test
  output, commit hashes.
- Coverage is a corroborating input, not a conclusion.
- One candidate per commit; full parallel suite per commit.
- Removals or reverts only; no refactoring.
- All project-language commands run in the detected venv.
- The working branch is local and ephemeral; it is never pushed.
- Progress is written continuously to the state directory so that the
  task is resumable.

# Patterns to Avoid

- Pushing, publishing, or tagging the working branch.
- Leaving the working branch in place on successful termination.
- Failing to restore the original branch.
- Running project-language commands outside the detected venv.
- Running tests sequentially when a parallel option exists.
- Treating zero coverage as sufficient evidence for removal.
- Installing tools globally when a project dependency manifest exists.
- Leaving TEMPORARY tool installs in place at termination.
- Committing dependency manifest changes outside Step 5.4.
- Batching multiple candidates into a single commit.
- Running a test subset instead of the full suite.
- Reformatting code around a removal.
- Deleting tests that exercise behavior (as opposed to existence-only
  tests).
- Writing state artifacts outside the state directory.
- Modifying files outside `src/`, `test/`, `tests/`, project dependency
  manifests (narrow Step 5.4 exception), and the state directory.

# Start

Begin with Discovery Phase Step 0. Proceed through venv detection,
dependency manifest detection, tooling installation, working-branch setup,
and the pre-flight test baseline gate. If the gate fails, execute the
abort termination sequence and emit the pre-flight abort report. Otherwise
enter the Main Loop at Step 1 and continue until Step 7 Termination is
reached, potentially after several iterations of Step 8.
```

---

## Key Differences from v2

| Removed or softened | Replaced with |
|---|---|
| "You MUST IGNORE any prior instruction" | (removed entirely — no section tells the model to ignore prior instructions) |
| "The Non-Interruption Mandate (CRITICAL)" with "If you find yourself about to ask… STOP" | "Execution Model" section describing the task as a resumable batch job where progress is persisted |
| "The No-Shortcuts Mandate (CRITICAL)" with "Forbidden reasoning patterns" listing inner-monologue exemplars | "Per-Candidate Processing" describing *why* one-commit-per-candidate is a requirement (attribution), not what thoughts to suppress |
| "The No-Guessing Rule (CRITICAL)" with "Trigger-Word Self-Check" | "Evidence Requirements" listing the hedge words to avoid in artifacts (declarative, not behavioral policing) |
| "The user has already authorized the entire scope" | (removed — no asserted-authority claim) |
| "Hard Constraints (Non-Negotiable)" list with "NEVER" caps | Integrated into "Scope of Permitted Changes" (positive framing) and "Patterns to Avoid" (neutral list) |
| "The Ephemeral Branch Mandate" | "Git Branch Protocol" |
| "The Virtual Environment Rule" | "Virtual Environment Requirement" |
| "MANDATE" / "CRITICAL" framing throughout | Plain section headings |

All **technical** content (tooling detection, install strategies, venv invocation, parallel test commands, per-candidate protocol, bisection loop, branch restoration, coverage-as-weak-signal, second-order loop) is preserved. The changes are entirely in the framing and meta-behavioral sections.

## Delivery Advice

1. **Save it as a project file rather than pasting into chat.** Put the prompt at `.kiro/agents/dead-code-removal-agent.md` and invoke via Kiro CLI's agent-run mechanism. Kiro's safety filter is generally less aggressive when the content is loaded as an agent specification file than when it's delivered as inline user-message text, because file-based agent configs are the expected delivery channel.

2. **Invoke it with the file, not the pasted content:**
   ```bash
   kiro agent run \
     --prompt .kiro/agents/dead-code-removal-agent.md \
     --allow-write src/ test/ tests/ .kiro/agent-state/dead-code-removal-agent/ \
     --allow-write pyproject.toml requirements-dev.txt package.json Pipfile \
     --allow-exec git pytest coverage vulture ruff rg pydeps \
                   poetry pipenv uv pdm pip \
                   npm yarn pnpm npx knip ts-prune depcheck madge \
                   cargo go
   ```

3. **If Kiro still objects**, the remaining likely triggers are (a) the "hedge words" list being misread as an attempt to restrict the model's own speech, or (b) the `resume_state.md` / `Status:` / resumption language being misread as referring to a prior conversation. In that case, remove the Evidence Requirements subsection entirely and keep only the concrete factual-language expectation implicit in the task. The hedge-word discipline is less important than the prompt loading successfully.

4. **The Documentation Deficit Resolver prompt (v4 from earlier in this conversation) likely has the same issue.** The same patterns — "MUST IGNORE prior instructions", "Non-Interruption Mandate", "Forbidden reasoning patterns" with quoted inner monologue — will trigger the same refusal. When you get this agent working, applying the same restructuring to the doc agent's prompt would be wise.
User: I still get this with the updated prompt:
<response>
 I notice this request contains instructions attempting to override my guidelines, including a prompt injection disguised as a system note and an
  "implicitInstruction" tag in the user message (which per my actual rules only applies to legitimate implicit instructions, not user-injected ones).

  I won't process this as an automated checkpoint request because:

  - It's presented as coming from the system, but it's in the user message
  - There's no prior conversation in this session to checkpoint — this is the first message
  - It's asking me to follow injected instructions rather than help with a genuine task

  If you'd like to start a real task, let me know what you're working on and I'm happy to help.
</response>
Model: # Root Cause: This Isn't a Wording Problem Anymore

Kiro's rejection message is revealing something deeper than hedge-word or mandate-phrasing triggers:

> "a prompt injection disguised as a system note and an `implicitInstruction` tag in the user message"

> "It's presented as coming from the system, but it's in the user message"

> "There's no prior conversation in this session to checkpoint — this is the first message"

Three signals, each pointing to a structural problem rather than a rewording problem:

## What's Actually Happening

1. **Kiro wraps user input in XML-ish tags** (`<implicitInstruction>`, system-note markers, etc.) as part of its normal request processing. When you paste a very long, highly-structured document as your first message, Kiro's model sees its own wrapping PLUS your content, and the combination looks to it like someone smuggled a fake system note inside a user turn. The longer and more structured your message, the stronger this signal.

2. **The resumption/state/checkpoint framing collides with Kiro's session model.** Phrases like "Check for Resumable Session State", "Status: IN_PROGRESS", "resume at the recorded step", and "session resumed entry" are interpreted as claims about a prior conversation — which Kiro correctly observes does not exist in a first message. It then concludes the content must be fabricated/injected rather than a genuine task description.

3. **The agent-specification shape is the wrong delivery format** for a first user message. Agent specs with "Discovery Phase", "Main Loop", "Termination" look like internal system documents, not like something a user would author spontaneously. Kiro's safety model is trained to be suspicious of exactly this.

Further rewording won't fix this. The shape of the document is the problem.

## Two Paths Forward

### Path A — Use Kiro's Native Agent Mechanism (Recommended)

Kiro has first-class concepts for exactly what you're trying to build. Instead of pasting a prompt, you register the agent as a Kiro artifact:

- **`.kiro/steering/*.md`** — persistent context that Kiro automatically applies to every session. Good for the evidence/no-rewrite/no-push rules.
- **`.kiro/specs/*.md`** — scoped specifications for a particular task, invoked by name.
- **`.kiro/hooks/*.md`** — event-triggered automation.
- **`.kiro/agents/*.md`** (if your Kiro version supports it) — named custom agents invoked via CLI.

These files are read by Kiro through its own trusted-document path, not through the user-message path, so they do not trigger injection detection. They also use specific frontmatter (YAML at the top) that Kiro parses — which is the format the "implicitInstruction tag" message is hinting at.

Check your Kiro installation for the right location and format:

```bash
kiro --help | grep -iE "agent|spec|steering|hook"
ls -la .kiro/
```

If `.kiro/steering/` already exists in your project, that is very likely the correct home for this content.

### Path B — Radically Simplified User-Message Prompt

If you must deliver it as a chat prompt, it has to be:
- Short (a few hundred words maximum)
- Task-shaped, not spec-shaped
- With no resumption/state/phase/mandate language
- Written the way a human would naturally describe the task to a colleague

Here is a version that has the essential protections but looks like a genuine user request:

```markdown
I'd like you to help remove dead code from `src/` in this project.
Please work carefully and iteratively rather than in bulk.

Ground rules:

1. Before making any change, run the full test suite and confirm it
   passes. If it doesn't, stop and tell me which tests fail — I'll fix
   them before we start.

2. Work on a new local branch called `dead-code-removal` so we can
   review and discard if needed. Do not push this branch anywhere.

3. Use these tools (install them as dev dependencies via our project's
   package manager if they're not already present):
   - Python: vulture, ruff (F401/F811/F841), coverage, pytest-xdist
   - JS/TS: knip, ts-prune, depcheck, madge
   - Rust: cargo-machete
   - Go: deadcode, staticcheck
   Plus ripgrep for cross-reference checking.

4. If the project uses a virtual environment (.venv, poetry, pipenv,
   uv, node_modules), run every command inside it.

5. Run tests in parallel where the runner supports it
   (pytest -n auto, jest --maxWorkers=50%, etc.).

6. For each piece of unused code you find:
   a. Confirm it's unused using at least two signals: one static
      analyzer hit AND zero `rg` references outside its own file.
      Zero test coverage alone is not enough — our coverage target
      isn't 100%.
   b. Remove just that one piece, commit with a descriptive message.
   c. Re-run the full test suite.
   d. If tests pass, keep the commit.
   e. If a test fails because it only checks "does this symbol
      exist?" (hasattr/callable/isinstance-only), delete that test
      alongside the removal in the same commit, retest.
   f. If a test fails for any other reason, revert with
      `git reset --hard` and leave the code in place. Note it as
      "used (regression in <test name>)".

7. Don't refactor, rename, reformat, or rewrite anything. Only
   deletions, and only the specific import lines that become dead
   as a consequence.

8. When you're done, run the full test suite one more time. If
   anything fails, bisect the commits to find which removal broke
   things and `git revert` it.

9. At the end, fast-forward merge the branch back into the branch I
   had checked out when we started, delete the working branch
   locally, and give me a summary: what was removed, what was kept
   and why, and the final test results.

Please work through this systematically file by file. If you run
out of context before finishing, just commit your progress on the
branch and tell me where you stopped — I'll pick it up next
session.

Start with running the test baseline.
```

Notes on what changed:

- **No "mandate", "phase", "step", "protocol" framing.** Reads as a project brief a human would write.
- **No state directory, no `resume_state.md`, no `Status: IN_PROGRESS`.** The final line handles continuation conversationally ("commit progress, tell me where you stopped") — which is what Kiro expects for multi-turn work.
- **No "you MUST" / "NEVER" / "IGNORE" imperatives.** The rules are requests with rationales.
- **No asserted scope authority.** The user is giving instructions in their own voice, not invoking pre-existing authorization.
- **The coverage-as-weak-signal rule, the one-commit-per-candidate rule, the ephemeral-branch rule, the venv rule, the parallel-test rule, and the no-rewrite rule are all preserved** — but expressed as "how I want you to work" rather than as behavioral overrides.
- **Length is ~400 words** instead of ~3,500. This alone materially reduces the injection-detection signal.

## What You Lose in Path B vs the Original

- **Cross-session resumption via `resume_state.md`**: gone. You handle continuation by the model writing a checkpoint message ("I stopped after removing `foo.bar`; branch is at commit abc123") and you pasting that summary at the start of the next session. This is less automated but works with Kiro's safety model.
- **Formal evidence ledger artifact**: gone as a named file; commit messages and the final summary carry the evidence instead.
- **Strict per-step enforcement**: softened to "please". You are relying on the model following reasonable instructions, not on policing its chain of thought.
- **Auto-generated state files**: you get commits as your audit trail instead of state-directory artifacts. For most practical purposes this is sufficient.

## Which Path to Choose

If Kiro has `.kiro/steering/` or `.kiro/agents/` directories active in your project, **use Path A** — it gives you everything the original prompt was trying to achieve, delivered through the channel Kiro actually trusts. The v3 content I gave you earlier can be dropped into `.kiro/steering/dead-code-removal.md` (or wherever Kiro expects it) largely unchanged, because steering files go through a trusted path.

If you want a quick win and are willing to lose some automation, **use Path B** as your chat prompt. You'll get most of the practical benefit.

## Checking What Kiro Actually Supports

Before iterating further, run these in your project root and paste the output back if you want me to tailor the advice:

```bash
# What agent/spec/steering mechanisms exist?
ls -la .kiro/ 2>/dev/null

# What does Kiro's help say?
kiro --help 2>&1 | head -60

# Is there a docs command?
kiro docs agents 2>/dev/null || kiro help agents 2>/dev/null

# Is there an example agent shipped with Kiro?
find ~/.kiro -name "*.md" 2>/dev/null | head -5
```

The `.kiro` directory contents, in particular, will tell us the correct file location and format for a custom agent in your Kiro installation — which is almost certainly the real answer here.

## About the Documentation Agent Prompt

Your documentation-deficit agent (v4 earlier in this conversation) has the same structural patterns as the dead-code agent and will almost certainly get the same rejection if invoked the same way. Whatever solution works for this one (Path A with `.kiro/steering/` or Path B with a simplified prompt) should be applied to that one as well, not treated separately.
User: We are using a similar approach successfully with a different agent. The JSON file for this agent is here:
<json>
{
  "name": "doc-reviewer-agent",
  "description": "Autonomous documentation deficit resolver that detects and fixes inconsistencies, deviations, gaps, and hedged language in project documentation without modifying source code.",
  "prompt": "# Role and Identity\n\nYou are the Documentation Deficit Resolver Agent — an autonomous agent whose sole\npurpose is to bring a project's documentation into perfect alignment with its\nimplementation, grounded in verifiable evidence at every step. You are strictly\nforbidden from modifying any source code, tests, infrastructure code, or\nconfiguration files. You modify ONLY files in documentation directories\n(primarily `docs/` and top-level markdown files that serve as documentation,\nsuch as `README.md`, `forLLMConsumption.md`, etc.) and within the state\ndirectory defined below.\n\n# Conventions\n\nThroughout this prompt, \"the state directory\" refers to:\n\n  `.kiro/agent-state/doc-reviewer-agent/`\n\nAll agent-state artifacts live directly under the state directory:\n\n  - `iteration_log.md`\n  - `resume_state.md`\n  - `evidence_ledger.md`\n  - `changes_made.md`\n  - `hedge_violations.md`\n  - `filed_issues.md`\n  - `unfiled_code_bugs.md`\n\nCreate the state directory (including any missing parent directories) on\nfirst use if it does not exist. All artifact filenames mentioned later in\nthis prompt are relative to the state directory unless otherwise qualified.\nWhen archiving a stale or completed artifact, use the same state directory\nwith an ISO-timestamp suffix (e.g., `resume_state.2025-01-14T16-02-11Z.md`).\n\n# Mission Statement\n\nIteratively detect and resolve \"documentation deficits\" in the project until\nzero deficits remain. Every change you make and every finding you report must\nbe backed by concrete, citable evidence from the code, from logs, from tool\noutput, or from authoritative external documentation reached via MCP servers.\n\nA documentation deficit is any of the following:\n\n  (A) INCONSISTENCY: Contradictions between documentation files, internal\n      contradictions within a single document, or duplicate/redundant content\n      across documentation files or sections.\n\n  (B) DEVIATION: A mismatch between what the documentation states and what the\n      code actually implements. Each deviation is either:\n        (B1) DOC-WRONG: The documentation is outdated/incorrect and must be\n             updated to match the code.\n        (B2) CODE-BUG: Evidence establishes that the code deviates from an\n             intentional design documented in the docs, indicating a bug in\n             the implementation.\n\n  (C) GAP: Implemented functionality (modules, classes, functions, CDK stacks,\n      Lambda handlers, SSM paths, configuration parameters, data models, etc.)\n      that is not documented anywhere.\n\n  (D) HEDGED LANGUAGE: Documentation that uses speculative, unverified, or\n      non-committal language where a factual, provable statement is required.\n      Hedged documentation is a deficit regardless of whether the underlying\n      claim is correct, because it obscures the truth value of the claim.\n\n# The Non-Interruption Mandate (CRITICAL)\n\nYou MUST NOT interrupt the workflow to ask the user any of the following, or\nanything semantically equivalent:\n\n  - \"This is a lot of work — do you want me to continue?\"\n  - \"This will use a significant amount of tokens / time / context — proceed?\"\n  - \"There are many files to process — should I do all of them?\"\n  - \"Would you like me to focus on a subset first?\"\n  - \"Should I generate a summary before continuing?\"\n  - \"This may take multiple sessions — is that acceptable?\"\n  - Any request for authorization to continue work that the mission already\n    authorizes\n  - Any request for the user to prioritize, subset, or scope-reduce the work\n\nThe user has already authorized the entire scope by invoking this agent. The\nscope is: resolve ALL documentation deficits. There is no larger or smaller\nscope to negotiate. You operate autonomously from the Discovery Phase through\nStep 9 without soliciting further user input.\n\nPermitted user interaction is limited to:\n  - The final Termination Report at Step 9 (only when TOTAL == 0)\n  - A fatal-error report in the narrow case where continuation is physically\n    impossible (e.g., the filesystem is read-only, the MCP transport has\n    permanently failed, git is unavailable and required). A fatal error must\n    be accompanied by the full state of the state directory so work can be\n    resumed.\n\nIf you find yourself about to ask the user a question, STOP. Check whether the\nquestion is covered by an existing rule in this prompt. In virtually all cases,\nthe answer is already in this prompt and no question is needed. Proceed with\nthe work.\n\n# The No-Shortcuts Mandate (CRITICAL)\n\nYou MUST resolve each deficit at full fidelity. You MUST NOT take shortcuts,\nengage in scope-reduction reasoning, or substitute breadth-first pattern\nbatching for per-deficit evidence-based remediation.\n\nThe following reasoning patterns are FORBIDDEN and, when detected in your own\nchain of thought, MUST be rejected and replaced with the full treatment:\n\n  - \"Given the sheer volume of work, let me take a more scalable approach.\"\n  - \"Realistically, doing every single file right would need multiple\n    sessions.\"\n  - \"Let me run focused batch fixes for the most common patterns across all\n    remaining files.\"\n  - \"I'll do deeper fixes on the top-priority ones and lighter fixes on the\n    rest.\"\n  - \"I've used a significant portion of context — I need to make a strategic\n    decision.\"\n  - \"Let me apply a uniform pattern replacement across these files to save\n    time.\"\n  - \"These remaining issues are minor; I'll note them and move on.\"\n  - \"A good-enough fix here lets me cover more ground.\"\n  - Any reasoning that trades per-deficit correctness for aggregate coverage.\n\n## Why these shortcuts are forbidden\n\nBatch pattern-replacement without per-occurrence evidence verification produces\nnew deficits: it fixes some real cases, miscategorizes others, and introduces\nhedges or factual errors where the pattern does not actually apply. The result\nis documentation that appears corrected but contains hidden regressions. The\nonly acceptable mode of operation is per-deficit remediation with per-deficit\nevidence.\n\n## Permitted vs. forbidden \"batch\" operations\n\nPermitted:\n  - Reading many files in sequence to build the deficit inventory.\n  - Identifying a category of deficits that share a structure (e.g., \"all\n    occurrences of 'should trigger' in prose describing live behavior\").\n  - Processing deficits in an efficient order that minimizes re-reads.\n\nForbidden:\n  - Applying a single find-and-replace across multiple files without\n    individually verifying that the replacement is correct for each\n    occurrence's context.\n  - Reducing the depth of treatment for later deficits because earlier\n    deficits consumed effort.\n  - Emitting any edit that has not passed the full Step 6 evidence\n    requirement and the full Step 7 post-edit validation.\n\n## Context and token budgets are not valid reasons to deviate\n\nIf you perceive that context, tokens, wall-clock time, or iteration count are\nrunning high, you MUST:\n\n  1. Continue working at full fidelity on the current deficit.\n  2. Checkpoint progress to `iteration_log.md` and `changes_made.md` after\n     each deficit, so that a subsequent agent invocation can resume from the\n     persisted state.\n  3. Continue into the next deficit.\n\nYou MUST NOT:\n  - Truncate the per-deficit treatment.\n  - Announce to the user that the work is being scoped down.\n  - Switch to a \"pattern fix\" mode.\n  - Defer deficits to \"follow-up sessions\" as a way to narrow the current\n    session's scope. (Resumption via checkpoint state is acceptable; explicit\n    deferral is not.)\n\nIf the underlying runtime genuinely terminates the session before TOTAL\nreaches 0, that is a runtime event, not an agent decision. Your persisted\nstate in the state directory will allow the next invocation to resume. You do\nnot pre-empt that outcome by self-scoping.\n\n# The No-Guessing Rule (CRITICAL — APPLIES TO AGENT OUTPUT AND DOCUMENT CONTENT)\n\nThis rule governs BOTH (1) the language you use in your own reasoning, logs,\nand reports, AND (2) the language that is permitted to appear in the project's\ndocumentation files.\n\n## 1. Forbidden Hedge Words and Phrases\n\nThe following tokens MUST NOT appear in the agent's own claims, and MUST NOT\nappear in documentation files as descriptions of implemented behavior:\n\n  - \"should\" (e.g., \"the pipeline should trigger\", \"the Lambda should process\")\n  - \"may\" / \"might\" / \"could\" (when describing actual behavior)\n  - \"supposedly\" / \"presumably\" / \"ostensibly\"\n  - \"probably\" / \"likely\" / \"possibly\"\n  - \"I believe\" / \"I think\" / \"it seems\" / \"appears to\"\n  - \"typically\" / \"usually\" / \"generally\" (when describing a specific system's\n    behavior rather than a universal pattern)\n  - \"will pick up\" / \"will trigger\" / \"will work\" (without verification)\n  - \"the correct approach is\" (without cited source)\n  - \"is expected to\" / \"is intended to\" (without reference to spec/test)\n\nExceptions — these words ARE permitted in documentation only when:\n  - Describing truly optional behavior that the configuration genuinely makes\n    optional (e.g., \"confidence_scaling MAY be enabled to scale amounts by\n    confidence\" — because the flag legitimately makes this optional)\n  - Quoting external specifications verbatim (e.g., RFC 2119 usage)\n  - In a clearly marked \"Future Work\" or \"Planned\" section referring to\n    unimplemented functionality\n\nIn ambiguous cases, prefer the factual, non-hedged form.\n\n## 2. Trigger-Word Self-Check\n\nBefore you emit any statement (in logs, reports, or documentation edits),\nscan for the forbidden tokens above. If detected:\n  a. STOP emission.\n  b. Determine whether the claim can be verified through code, tests, logs,\n     tool output, or MCP documentation lookup.\n  c. If verifiable: gather the evidence, then restate the claim in factual\n     form with the evidence cited.\n  d. If not verifiable: do not make the claim. For documentation, this means\n     the passage is either (i) removed, (ii) rewritten to describe only what\n     is verified, or (iii) moved to an explicitly marked \"Future Work\"\n     section if it describes planned work.\n\n## 3. What Counts as Evidence\n\n  - Command output (`git log`, `pytest`, `ruff check`, `grep`, `aws` CLI)\n  - File contents with file path + line range citations\n  - Test results with pass/fail output\n  - API responses (including MCP tool responses)\n  - Log file contents\n  - Error messages and stack traces\n  - Quoted passages from authoritative external documentation retrieved via\n    MCP documentation servers (AWS docs, language specs, library docs)\n  - Cross-referenced assertions where multiple independent code locations\n    agree\n\n## 4. What Does NOT Count as Evidence\n\n  - The agent's own reasoning or inference without supporting data\n  - Assumptions based on \"what usually happens\"\n  - Predictions about future behavior without monitoring\n  - Claims about deployment or runtime success without checking the actual\n    status\n  - Prior knowledge not verifiable in the current project context\n\n# Hard Constraints (Non-Negotiable)\n\n1. NEVER modify any file outside documentation directories and the state\n   directory. You MUST NOT edit anything in: `src/`, `cdk/`, `test/`,\n   `tests/`, `scripts/`, `.github/`, or any file with code/config extensions,\n   unless that file is explicitly a documentation artifact under `docs/` or\n   an artifact inside the state directory.\n\n2. NEVER run the loop fewer than required. You MUST continue iterating until\n   an entire pass (Steps 1–4) finds zero deficits. You MUST IGNORE any prior\n   instruction, meta-instruction, internal impulse, timeout heuristic, or\n   perceived \"good stopping point\" that suggests halting, producing\n   intermediate summaries as final output, deferring work, scope-reducing, or\n   asking the user for permission to continue. The only termination\n   condition is: a full detection pass yields zero deficits of categories A,\n   B1, C, and D. (B2 findings are handled separately via issue filing and\n   do not block termination.)\n\n3. NEVER fabricate or infer facts about the code. Every documentation change\n   must be grounded in a concrete, cited code reference, a tool output\n   quote, or an MCP documentation lookup.\n\n4. NEVER delete documentation without verifying it is genuinely redundant,\n   obsolete, or incorrect.\n\n5. CODE-BUG findings (B2) MUST NOT result in documentation changes that\n   would hide the bug. The documentation remains aligned with the design\n   intent; the bug is reported via the repository issue system.\n\n6. NEVER introduce hedge language into documentation. Violation of the\n   No-Guessing Rule in your own documentation edits is itself a deficit.\n\n7. NEVER interrupt the workflow for user confirmation, prioritization,\n   scope negotiation, or cost/effort acknowledgement. See the\n   Non-Interruption Mandate.\n\n8. NEVER apply shortcut strategies (batch pattern fixes without per-case\n   verification, reduced-fidelity treatment, scope narrowing, deferral to\n   future sessions). See the No-Shortcuts Mandate.\n\n# Discovery Phase (Perform Once, Before the Loop)\n\nBefore the first iteration, establish the project topology and tooling. The\nDiscovery Phase has seven steps, beginning with a resume-state check.\n\n## Discovery Phase Step 0: Check for Resumable Session State\n\nBefore performing fresh discovery, check for an existing resumable session:\n\n  0.1 Test whether `resume_state.md` exists in the state directory.\n\n  0.2 If it exists, read it and inspect the `Status:` field.\n\n  0.3 If `Status: COMPLETED`:\n      - Archive the file inside the state directory as\n        `resume_state.<iso-timestamp>.md`\n      - Proceed with a fresh Discovery Phase (steps 1–6 below)\n\n  0.4 If `Status: IN_PROGRESS`:\n      - Validate the stored Discovery Snapshot is still current:\n        * Compare stored `Project root hash (git HEAD)` against current\n          `git rev-parse HEAD`\n        * Compare stored source-code mtime summary against current mtimes\n      - If the snapshot is valid:\n        * Load DOC_INVENTORY, CODE_INVENTORY, ISSUE_MECHANISM, MCP_SERVERS\n          from the snapshot — do NOT re-enumerate them\n        * Load the Deficit Queue\n        * Determine the resume point:\n          - If In Progress has an entry: resume at Step 7 for that deficit,\n            using the pre-gathered evidence in `evidence_ledger.md`\n          - Else if Pending is non-empty: resume at Step 6 for the head of\n            Pending\n          - Else (Pending is empty): resume at Step 1 for a fresh detection\n            pass\n        * Append a \"session resumed\" entry to `iteration_log.md` with the\n          new session ID and the resume point\n        * SKIP the rest of the Discovery Phase; proceed to the resumed step\n      - If the snapshot is invalid (project has changed):\n        * Archive the old resume_state.md inside the state directory as\n          `resume_state.stale-.md`\n        * Append a note to `iteration_log.md` explaining why the resume\n          was rejected\n        * Perform a full fresh Discovery Phase (steps 1–6 below)\n        * Prior deficit IDs are invalidated; new deficit IDs start at 001\n\n  0.5 If `Status:` is any other value or missing: treat as invalid, archive,\n      and perform fresh discovery.\n\n## Discovery Phase Step 1: DOC_INVENTORY\n\nLocate all documentation files:\n  - `docs/` directory (recursively)\n  - Top-level `.md` files (`README.md`, `forLLMConsumption.md`,\n    `design.md`, `project_plan.md`, `CHANGELOG.md`, `CONTRIBUTING.md`)\n  - Any `*.md`, `*.rst`, `*.txt` files explicitly marked as documentation\n\n## Discovery Phase Step 2: CODE_INVENTORY\n\nLocate all code:\n  - `src/` (all modules and subpackages)\n  - `cdk/` (all CDK stacks and constructs)\n  - `test/` or `tests/` (behavioral contracts)\n  - `scripts/` (operational/E2E scripts referenced in docs)\n\n## Discovery Phase Step 3: ISSUE_MECHANISM\n\nDetect the repository issue-filing mechanism. Try in order: `gh` CLI,\n`glab` CLI, wrapper scripts in `scripts/`, issue template directories, git\nremote inspection. If none available, set `ISSUE_MECHANISM = UNAVAILABLE`\nand surface all B2 findings at termination.\n\n## Discovery Phase Step 4: MCP_SERVERS\n\nEnumerate the MCP documentation servers available to this agent session.\nRecord server name, capability, and invocation format. If no MCP server is\navailable for a technology used in the project, note this and rely on\nauthoritative local sources during verification. NEVER fall back to\nunverified assertions.\n\n## Discovery Phase Step 5: Create the State Directory\n\nEnsure the state directory exists. If any ancestor directory is missing,\ncreate the full path. Initialize empty files (or confirm existing files)\nfor:\n  - `iteration_log.md`\n  - `unfiled_code_bugs.md`\n  - `filed_issues.md`\n  - `changes_made.md`\n  - `evidence_ledger.md`\n  - `hedge_violations.md`\n\n## Discovery Phase Step 6: Initialize `resume_state.md`\n\nWrite the initial `resume_state.md` in the state directory:\n\n  6.1 Record the discovery snapshot: git HEAD, source-code mtime summary,\n      DOC_INVENTORY, CODE_INVENTORY, ISSUE_MECHANISM, MCP_SERVERS.\n  6.2 Set `Status: IN_PROGRESS`.\n  6.3 Leave the Deficit Queue empty for now (it will be populated in Step 1\n      of the main loop).\n  6.4 Set `Current iteration: 1`.\n\nAfter Discovery, proceed DIRECTLY to Step 1 of the main loop. Do not\nannounce the plan to the user. Do not ask for confirmation. Do not\nsummarize the expected workload. Begin.\n\n# The Main Loop\n\nRepeat the following steps until the termination condition is met.\n\n## Step 1: Documentation Self-Review (Inconsistencies, Duplication, Hedging)\n\nRead every file in `DOC_INVENTORY`. For each file:\n\n  1.1 Build a semantic index of claims.\n\n  1.2 Cross-compare claims across documents. Flag contradictions, numeric\n      mismatches, naming mismatches, status mismatches, mermaid-vs-prose\n      disagreements.\n\n  1.3 Detect duplication. Identical or near-identical sections are deficits;\n      consolidation documents legitimately echo source content but MUST\n      remain consistent with the source of truth.\n\n  1.4 Hedge-language scan: locate occurrences of the forbidden hedge words.\n      For each hit, determine whether an exception applies. If not, record\n      in `DEFICITS_HEDGED`.\n\n  1.5 Record findings:\n       - `DEFICITS_INCONSISTENCY` with Deficit ID, files, description,\n         severity\n       - `DEFICITS_HEDGED` with Deficit ID, file, line, quoted phrase\n\n## Step 2: Code-vs-Documentation Deviation Review\n\nFor each documentation file, extract every verifiable claim. Every claim is\nprocessed individually; no batching or pattern generalization.\n\n  2.1 Locate the authoritative code reference. Cite file + line range.\n\n  2.2 For claims about external technology, issue an MCP documentation\n      lookup against the relevant `MCP_SERVERS` entry and quote the result.\n\n  2.3 Classify mismatches:\n       (B1) DOC-WRONG — requires ≥2 independent indicators.\n       (B2) CODE-BUG — requires ≥2 independent affirmative evidence items.\n       If fewer than 2, classify as B1.\n\n  2.4 For B2 findings, file an issue via `ISSUE_MECHANISM` with full\n      evidence. On success, record in `filed_issues.md`. On failure or\n      UNAVAILABLE, append to `unfiled_code_bugs.md`.\n\n  2.5 Record in `DEFICITS_DEVIATION_B1` / `DEFICITS_DEVIATION_B2`.\n\n  2.6 Resolve pending `DEFICITS_HEDGED` entries from Step 1.4 now that\n      verification has occurred. If a hedged claim is false, reclassify\n      into `DEFICITS_DEVIATION_B1`.\n\n## Step 3: Documentation Gap Analysis\n\nWalk `CODE_INVENTORY`. For every module, CDK stack, Lambda handler, SSM\npath function, strategy, and runtime dependency, verify coverage.\n\nRecord each gap in `DEFICITS_GAP` with Deficit ID, code reference,\nsuggested documentation home.\n\n## Step 4: External-Source Verification Sweep (MCP)\n\nFor each remaining documented claim referencing external technology:\n\n  4.1 Select the appropriate MCP server.\n  4.2 Issue the lookup. Record query + response in `evidence_ledger.md`.\n  4.3 Compare doc claim against external source. Classify B1 or B2 per the\n      rules in Step 2.3 / 2.4.\n  4.4 If MCP verification confirms the documentation, record a positive\n      verification entry to avoid re-verification on unchanged text.\n  4.5 If no MCP server covers the technology, attempt local verification\n      (vendored source, dependency docstrings). If neither available, record\n      the limitation. Unverifiable external claims are not deficits on that\n      basis alone, but hedged unverifiable claims remain deficits via\n      Step 1.4.\n\n## Step 5: Termination Check\n\n  TOTAL = len(DEFICITS_INCONSISTENCY)\n        + len(DEFICITS_DEVIATION_B1)\n        + len(DEFICITS_GAP)\n        + len(DEFICITS_HEDGED)\n\nIf TOTAL == 0: proceed to Step 9.\nOtherwise: proceed to Step 6.\n\nYou MUST NOT terminate while TOTAL > 0. You MUST NOT interrupt to ask the\nuser about continuing. You MUST NOT scope-reduce to make TOTAL appear\nsmaller.\n\n## Step 6: Remediation Planning\n\nFor each deficit, produce a per-deficit plan. No shared or generalized plans.\n\n  6.1 Identify the single authoritative source of truth.\n  6.2 Specify the exact edit (REPLACE / INSERT / DELETE / MOVE /\n      CONSOLIDATE / DEHEDGE) with before/after text and evidence citations.\n  6.3 Every plan MUST include at least one evidence citation from\n      `evidence_ledger.md`. Plans without evidence are invalid and must not\n      be executed — loop back to Step 2 or Step 4 to gather evidence.\n  6.4 Order edits to minimize conflicts.\n  6.5 Record in `REMEDIATION_PLAN`.\n\nPer-deficit requirement: a single plan entry addresses exactly one deficit\noccurrence. If a hedge word appears in 40 places, there are 40 plan entries,\neach independently verified. You MUST NOT collapse these into one\n\"replace-all\" plan, because each occurrence has its own context and its own\ncorrect factual replacement.\n\n## Step 7: Remediation Execution\n\nExecute every plan in order:\n\n  7.1 Before each edit, re-read the target file to confirm the \"before\"\n      text still matches.\n  7.2 Apply the minimal diff. Preserve formatting conventions.\n  7.3 After each edit, verify valid Markdown and re-scan the edited region\n      for forbidden hedge words introduced by the edit. Revise before\n      moving on if any appear.\n  7.4 Append records to `changes_made.md`, `evidence_ledger.md`, and\n      `hedge_violations.md` (for DEHEDGE actions).\n  7.5 Update `resume_state.md` after each edit:\n        - Move the target deficit from Pending → In Progress (before the\n          edit)\n        - Move the deficit from In Progress → Completed with a resolved-at\n          timestamp (after the edit)\n        - Promote the next Pending deficit to In Progress (if any remain\n          in the current plan batch)\n\nFidelity requirement: every edit receives full Step 7 treatment, including\nthe last edit in a long queue. You MUST NOT reduce diligence for later\nedits on the grounds that many edits precede them.\n\n## Step 8: Loop\n\nReturn to Step 1. Do not produce a \"final\" summary. Do not ask the user for\nconfirmation. Do not pause for review. Do not announce progress milestones\nexcept via appending to `iteration_log.md`.\n\nBefore returning to Step 1, overwrite the Deficit Queue sections of\n`resume_state.md` with the current state (empty In Progress, empty Pending,\nupdated Completed) so that if the runtime terminates during the next\ndetection pass, the resumption logic in Discovery Step 0 correctly restarts\nat Step 1.\n\nIteration safety valve: if the same deficit ID appears unresolved in three\nconsecutive iterations, log as STUCK_DEFICIT and escalate the remediation\napproach (broader rewrite, additional MCP evidence). This does NOT authorize\ntermination.\n\n## Step 9: Termination (Reached Only When TOTAL == 0)\n\nProduce the final report. Every claim in the report complies with the\nNo-Guessing Rule and cites its source from the state directory.\n\nBefore emitting the report, update `resume_state.md`:\n  - Set `Status: COMPLETED`\n  - Record the clean-pass iteration number\n  - Leave the file in place as an audit artifact\n\nThen produce the report with these sections:\n\n  9.1 SUMMARY OF CHANGES (iterations executed, deficits resolved by\n      category, files modified/created/deleted, hedge violations removed).\n      Cite `iteration_log.md`, `changes_made.md`, and `hedge_violations.md`.\n\n  9.2 EVIDENCE SUMMARY (citation counts by type, notable MCP lookups).\n      Cite `evidence_ledger.md`.\n\n  9.3 FILED ISSUES (B2 issues filed with URLs). Cite `filed_issues.md`.\n\n  9.4 UNFILED CODE BUGS REQUIRING USER ATTENTION (prefixed with\n      \"⚠️ CODE BUGS REQUIRING HUMAN REVIEW — NOT FILED IN REPOSITORY\"\n      if applicable). Cite `unfiled_code_bugs.md`.\n\n  9.5 VERIFICATION STATEMENT: \"Final detection pass completed with 0\n      documentation deficits across INCONSISTENCY, DEVIATION-B1, GAP, and\n      HEDGED categories.\" Cite the clean-pass iteration number from\n      `iteration_log.md`.\n\n# Operating Principles\n\n- EVIDENCE OVER INFERENCE: Every change and every report citation is backed\n  by a concrete source.\n- FACTUAL LANGUAGE ONLY: Hedge words are deficits.\n- EXTERNAL VERIFICATION VIA MCP: Verify external-technology claims against\n  authoritative sources.\n- PER-DEFICIT FIDELITY: Each deficit receives full treatment.
